The product-experience brief ([09-product-experience.md](../product/09-product-experience.md), stream W1) and the screen designs (stream W2) are produced now, so the product experience is designed and decided early; the brief's open product decisions go to the founder. `web/` code stays at M9 as [ADR-0001](../adr/0001-engineering-setup.md) ES-01 plans, unless the founder decides otherwise. No web framework is decided: [HLD §11](../HLD.md#11-technology) names Next.js / React Native, which is not yet ratified as a decision (the brief's PX-14 recommends ratifying or revisiting it at the start of M8) | Accepted (founder, 2026-09-27, in chat): "there is no reason to not start working on web as well. So we can design early and decide product experience." | Designing the screens early finds product decisions and spec gaps (the brief's §6 and §7) while the specs are still changing, instead of at M9 when the web app is on the Phase 1 critical path | Start web design at M9 with the web app (the milestone plan) |
| DEC-135 | The fourteen product decisions of the web brief (PX-1 to PX-14) are accepted as recommended ([09-product-experience.md §6](../product/09-product-experience.md#6-open-product-decisions-for-the-founder)): PX-1 (b), the confirmation screen shows the summary and dollar figures on top and every value below, with proposals, warnings, and `auto` expanded; PX-2 (b), per-section confirmation with each proposed value ticked or edited, no bulk accept; PX-3 (b), one Stop control offering Pause, then the owner Stop, then the kill switch; PX-4 (b), no step-up to pause, step-up to resume and to Stop; PX-5 (b), a reconciliation hold shows the difference and what still runs, and resume needs step-up once the difference is shown; PX-6 (b), notifications carry generic text only, with an opaque platform-assigned agent label as an owner option; PX-7 (b), a generic notification, the deadline on the detail screen, escalation re-notifies, and step-up per live approval; PX-8 (a), promote to live creates a new live mandate prefilled from the paper one and confirmed in full; PX-9 (a), the research agent is hidden from users until the DEC-99 evaluation passes; PX-10 (b), Approve and Skip have equal weight and neither is preselected; PX-11 (b), operators and admins may pause, stop, and flatten, and approvers may pause; PX-12 (b), "Stop all agents on this account" is the default and "Close everything on this account" a second listed choice; PX-13 (a), recorded as DEC-136 because it changes DEC-131; PX-14 (c), ratify or revisit the web stack at the start of M8 | Accepted (founder, 2026-09-27) | The founder, in chat on 2026-09-27: "accept all on 155" (#155 merged the brief with DEC-134). Each recommendation was already the more conservative option the designs followed (the brief's §6), so the designs change only for PX-13 | Decide each question separately, or leave them open until the designs exist |

Under PX-13 (a) ([09-product-experience.md §6](../product/09-product-experience.md#6-open-product-decisions-for-the-founder)), Stop is offered only when the agent is flat; otherwise the owner's choice is the kill switch (close and stop) or "stop and release positions to me" (as `release`: the positions become the owner's and unprotected, with the warning shown, and step-up). The runtime change is implemented by a later E6-5 change after #151 merges, not inside #151; until then the runtime keeps DEC-131 item (10)'s behaviour, and the brief's §7 question 1 covers that interval | Accepted (founder, 2026-09-27); amends DEC-131 | The founder accepted every recommendation of the brief ("accept all on 155", DEC-135), and PX-13 (a) changes an accepted decision, so it needs its own row (AGENTS.md rule 9). Every ending of an agent then has a defined owner of the positions; under DEC-131's rule nobody re-places protection when the GTC orders expire, and the brief's §7 question 1 stays open | Keep DEC-131 as it stands (PX-13 (b)); Stop always flattens (PX-13 (c)) |
| DEC-137 | Three stricter rules in the `xtask` gates, each closing a hole a merged PR went through, and none of them looser than what it replaces. **(1) The pending gate reads the failure.** A pending test must not only fail but fail *at its story's stub*: its output must carry the stub's own report — the `Debug` or `Display` of an `Unimplemented` error, or its `code()` — matched as a whole word for the one-word forms. A story id is not evidence, because a test can write one into its own assertion message, and a stub may name another story (`E6-1` under a `pending E6-5` test); nor is any other error a crate's stubs happen to return. A failure on a fixture, parse, or harness panic fails the gate, naming the test and the panic it started with, and a test the run reports `ignored` counts as one that did not run rather than one that failed. Where a crate's implementation lands in stages, a pending test for a later story fails on the answer the implemented part gives rather than at a stub: `mandate-risk`'s E6-3 spine (#157) decides an exit without E6-6's session rules or E6-8's pacing, and AGENTS.md rule 13 forbids it to refuse an exit at all. Those tests are named one by one in `BEHAVIOUR_ONLY_TESTS`, never by crate, DEC-110's rule still holds for each (it must run and must fail), and a row whose test is no longer pending fails the gate, so the exception expires with the story it waits for. This closes gap 1 only in part: the gate reads a failure's text, not its cause, so a marker in a test's own assertion message satisfies it, and `assert!(cond, "msg")` and `.expect("msg")` panic exactly as `panic!("msg")` does, which leaves no textual rule to separate them. Reading the cause is **E1-3**, and the tracker's row stays open until it lands (the coordinator's round-2 ruling). A stub that reports nothing is fixed, not excused: `mandate-num`'s E4-2 stubs returned `NumError::Overflow`, which no reader can tell from real arithmetic, and `mandate-spec`'s `Confirmation::update` returned a bare `false`; the first gains a `NumError::Unimplemented`, the second returns `Result<bool, SpecError>`, which is what a fold step that cannot be decided owes its caller anyway. **(2) A pending test is written out as a plain function.** `cargo xtask markers` fails on an `#[ignore = "pending <story>"]` inside a `macro_rules!` body, on a `$`-named function, or on no named function of its own (a marker forwarded to a macro call), because the source scan cannot turn any of them into a runnable test name; a plain function inside a macro call, as `proptest!` writes, is fine. **(3) A crate with pending tests runs the mutation gate** (amends [DEC-83](#decisions), which skipped it entirely): `cargo mutants --in-diff` covers it, and a missed mutant fails the gate unless the function it mutates is a stub in the post-change source — argument discards (`let _ = ...;`) and a last statement returning an `Unimplemented` error, the only stub form this workspace allows, since `Cargo.toml` denies `clippy::todo` and `clippy::unimplemented` — which is named as skipped. The exemption applies only to a run that exits with the missed-mutant status (2) over an output directory deleted before the run and written after the diff that run read, so a run that stopped before testing anything, a build failure, or a restored cache keeps its failure; a failed baseline, a timeout, and every other summary still fail. **Amended by [DEC-139](#decisions):** before the run the gate counts each mutated package's live tests and fails a package that has none, because such a package reports every mutant caught while nothing ran, which is how this rule first judged `mandate-builder` ([#175](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/175)) | Accepted (agent, under DEC-79) | Each rule is a correction moved to the highest rung that holds it (AGENTS.md's trust ladder). (1) The gate proved a pending test fails, not what it fails on, so a test that can never pass on correct code passed it (#118, #134). (2) The scan reads `fn <name>` after the marker, so it found 85 of `mandate-risk`'s 105 pending tests before #136 wrote its twenty reference cases out as plain functions. (3) DEC-83 skipped a crate for as long as it had pending tests, so a multi-PR implementation stack was never mutation-gated in CI and #157, #158 and #163 ran the gate by hand. Each change only adds a failure mode: no check accepts anything it rejected before | (1) Accept a story id anywhere in the output, which a test satisfies with its own message; accept any error a crate's stubs return, which lets an always-`Err` function widen the accepted text and leaves the stub unreadable to a person; require the error's `Debug` rendering only, which would fail the 84 `mandate-runtime` pending tests that report through `Display`. (2) Read the expanded test list from `cargo nextest list` rather than the source, which costs the file and line the gate reports. (3) Keep the crate-level skip and rely on the implementation PR; mark every stub `#[cfg_attr(test, mutants::skip)]`, which needs a new dependency |
| DEC-138 | The tracer bullet (E7-7), the thinnest end-to-end path that places one order on the owner's Alpaca **paper** account through the real crates ([task brief](tasks/E7-7-tracer-bullet.md)), where no document says where the shell that binds runtime, executor, and connector lives or how a half-built path must behave. (1) **A new safety-critical crate, `mandate-shell`, at layer 8** (`pure = false`, `allowed_external = ["clap", "thiserror", "tokio"]`, with a CODEOWNERS line), holding the process shell, the stage adapters, the effect runner, the host controls, and a `mandate-tracer` binary. **This amends DEC-133 item 1**, which places the `IntentSink` adapter "in the layer-7 shell" (AGENTS.md rule 9): the adapter moves to layer 8 with the rest of the shell, for the reason below; nothing else in DEC-133 changes, and stream K's own rows still say layer 7 until stream K or the coordinator corrects them. Layer 8 is new and is the lowest layer that works: `mandate-backtest` (E4-2's moving-average baseline, the tracer's signal) is at layer 7, a crate may depend only on strictly lower layers, and the shell must also see `mandate-runtime`, `mandate-executor`, and `mandate-alpaca` at layer 6; `xtask/layers.toml` already states the same rule in its note that the CLI moves up when a command needs `mandate-backtest`. (2) **Not a `mandate-cli` subcommand**: `mandate-cli` is `safety_critical = false`, so the order path there would be either unmarked (a regression in verification, which the founder's condition forbids) or would drag `download`, `inspect`, `journal verify`, and `artifact` under the safety-critical header and the mutation gate; the audit tool also should not hold the paper credentials, and it would have to move to layer 8 anyway. (3) **The shell holds no trading logic** — no sizing, no gating, no pricing, no state machine, and no arithmetic on money or quantity; a source scan and the enumerated `allowed_external` hold that. (4) **Fail-closed is the story's verification condition**: every stage that can add risk maps every `Unimplemented`, error, absence, ambiguity, or timeout to no order, and every stage that reduces risk (the flatten planner, the protective sequence) maps it to **refuse to start**, because an empty exit plan is an unprotected position, not a safe default (AGENTS.md rules 3 and 13). One stage does not fail with an error at all, and the reading for it is the substance of this item: (a) the risk gate needs nothing added, because it already fails closed for adding risk — an owed §9.1 check is recorded `NotReached` and an opening the implemented checks would allow returns `Err(GateError::Unimplemented("evaluate", story))` (DEC-129 item 29, `gate.rs`'s `owed()` on #160's head `9ae15c2`), which the shell's error mapping already denies; the shell therefore holds **no list of which story owns which check**, because that would be gating knowledge in a layer-8 shell against item 3 and would drift from `owed()`, and its only addition is to refuse an opening `Allow` whose `checks` carry a `NotReached`, derived from the gate's own output. An earlier revision of this row claimed the gate passes an unowned check and answers `Allow`; that was read off the superseded head `eca4af4` and is false, and the correction is recorded rather than quietly dropped because the wrong reading nearly moved gating into the shell; (b) `mandate_runtime::ports::FlattenPlanner::plan` is **infallible**, so the exit stage is held by a startup probe of `mandate_risk::agent_flatten` plus a poisoned adapter that halts and alerts, never by an empty or invented plan, and the coordinator ruled for that probe and halt over a change to `mandate-runtime`'s port, so `plan` stays infallible and the poison flag is rung-3 enforcement held by a test, not rung 1. (5) **No mapping may permit**: each mapping is a total function with no permitting arm, and `crates/mandate-shell/src/` contains no `_ =>`, `unwrap_or`, `unwrap_or_default`, `unwrap_or_else`, or `ok()` **anywhere in the crate**, not only on a gate or plan result. `DryRunVerdict` belongs to `mandate-runtime`, which this stream leaves unchanged, so nothing is claimed about that type's `Default`; a shell test over the mapping function holds the property. (6) **The stage list is an enum** and the fail-closed suite's cases are built by an exhaustive match over it, so a new stage does not compile until it has a fail-closed case. (7) **The tracer lands with every stage stubbed**, asserting zero orders in the state the repository is actually in, and each upstream stream's merge flips one adapter; the end-to-end happy path carries the single `#[ignore = "pending E7-7"]` marker, which the pending gate proves fails today through the upstream `Unimplemented`. The per-stage fail-closed cases carry no marker and pass today, and each is built with the **other** stages as permissive doubles rather than as they stand, because with every stage stubbed the first refusal masks the rest and "zero submissions" is true of any bug while `mandate_executor::handle` is itself a stub; the assertion is made at the furthest boundary the failing stage could reach (the `IntentSink` counter for steps 1 to 8), and an "all doubles, no stub" case proves the harness can place an order at all. The suite therefore lives in a `#[cfg(test)]` module inside `src/`, since an integration test is compiled without `cfg(test)` and a feature-gated double could be switched on in a build that ships. (8) **Paper only, and the controls are the ones that exist**: `AlpacaPaperHttp` holds no base URL — every request is `format!("{PAPER_HOST}{path}")` from a client built `https_only(true)` with `redirect::Policy::none()`, behind `is_paper_trading_path` — so a comparison of "the transport's base" with `PAPER_HOST` would test a constant against itself and is not a control. The rung-1 control is instead the shell's `allowed_external`, which names **no HTTP client**, so nothing in `mandate-shell` can implement `TradingTransport` or open a connection, and `cargo xtask layers` fails if that list grows. What the binary does check is an *input*: there is no `--host` argument, no host environment variable, and no config file, and an `ALPACA`-named variable holding a URL is a refusal with `non_paper_host`. (9) **Nothing is sent without two explicit flags**, `--confirm-paper` and `--place-one-order`; the default plans, journals the decision, and stops before the submission. (10) **AAPL, one share**, from the research basket (DEC-90): common stock, so the eligibility floor has no ETP-class case; above 1.00 USD, so one tick rule; already downloadable. The mandate fixture's allocation and notional cap are recomputed from the rules so the sizer clips to exactly one share, and the fixture is validated with zero violations before any other test runs. It is internal test data, not a product instrument choice (AGENTS.md rule 11). (11) **CI never touches a network**: the Alpaca scenarios are recorded fixtures in stream K's shape through an injected transport, `AlpacaPaperHttp` is never constructed in a test, the bar dataset is written by `mandate-marketdata`'s own writer, and the clock and `IdGen` are injected so the golden journal is byte-stable. (11a) `mandate_journal::verify_events` is **not** the oracle for the paper environment: its `columns_match` only checks that the stored column equals the body's field, so a consistently `Live` journal verifies, and a separate scanner parses every committed draft and asserts `Environment::Paper`. (11b) A restart must send **zero** further submissions, not merely be deduped: `Input::Started` queries and never resubmits (DEC-133 item 2), and an `IdGen` reset to its seed re-derives the same id, which the broker's `DuplicateClientOrderId` would hide from any assertion that tolerates a second request. (12) **No status PR and no reference case moves**: the tracer cites RC-04, RC-09, RC-09B, RC-11, RC-14, RC-16, RC-17, the mandate gate and autonomy families, and the journal append vectors read-only, and a behaviour none of them covers is a finding for that stream's case file, not a case here. (13) **`Ask` resolves to skip**, journaled: there is no escalation until M7, and a half-built escalation that auto-approves is the defect this stream exists to prevent. (14) **Running twice never buys twice**: the tracer refuses with `cycle_already_open` when the agent stream carries an open order or a position in the instrument, overridable only by an explicit `--new-cycle`, and a fresh journal against a position the broker holds is a reconciliation mismatch that pauses and alerts rather than a clean start | Accepted (agent, under DEC-79); amends DEC-133 item 1 | The founder approved the tracer bullet on 2026-09-27 with one condition, no regression in verification, and these are the engineering readings that condition forces. Items 4(a), 4(b), 7, 8, 11a and 11b are the independent review's findings 1 to 6 on the brief PR: the first draft assumed every unbuilt stage would fail with `Unimplemented`, and three of them instead pass, allow, or return an infallible empty value, which is the class of defect the tracer exists to find. None touches live money, spending, legal text, or a safety invariant: the path is paper only and adds checks rather than replacing any. Items 1 and 2 are the only founder-owned surface (`xtask/layers.toml` and CODEOWNERS), named in the brief's Decisions needed for veto | A `mandate-cli tracer` subcommand (rejected: safety-critical marking and credential blast radius); a throwaway example or integration-test-only harness (rejected: M7's soak and M9's deployment need this shell anyway, and a throwaway would not be safety-critical); waiting for every stream to land before wiring anything (rejected: that is exactly the Phase 1 soak surprise the founder asked to avoid); treating an unimplemented stage as a no-op (rejected: indistinguishable from allow at the exit stages) |
| DEC-139 | **The mutation gate may not count a mutant as caught unless a test ran against it.** `cargo mutants` tests each mutant with `cargo nextest run --package=<the mutated crate>`, and a package whose every test is `#[ignore]`d answers that with `0 tests run … error: no tests to run` and a non-zero exit; `cargo-mutants` 27.1.0 classifies a mutant on the test child's exit status alone, has no outcome for "nothing ran" and no `--no-tests` option of its own, so it records every such mutant as **caught**. So [DEC-137](#decisions) item 3, which put a crate with pending tests under the gate, judged the first crate it met by an exit code rather than by a test: on [#175](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/175) all 30 `mandate-builder` mutants came back caught while nothing had run, and `BuilderError::code` returning `""` survived all 1024 live tests in the workspace. The gate now gathers that evidence itself: after it writes the diff and before it runs, it lists the mutants the run would test (`cargo mutants --list --json --in-diff`, no build) and counts each of those packages' live, not-`#[ignore]`d tests (`cargo nextest list --message-format json`), and a package with mutants and no live test **fails the gate by name** — "no live tests to judge N mutant(s); it needs at least one live test before the gate can judge any of them, a crate of nothing but stubs included" — rather than being skipped. A package the nextest listing does not mention has no live tests, which is the same verdict as a package whose every test is ignored. The count is per package because that is the package `cargo mutants` runs tests from; were a workspace-wide test run ever configured, the count could only be stricter than it needs to be, never looser. This adds one failure mode and changes nothing else: the run, its exit-code reading, and DEC-137 item 3's stub exemption are untouched, so nothing `main` rejects is accepted (the founder's no-regression condition). **The gate's own wiring is under a test, not only an experiment** (the coordinator's ruling on the independent review, finding 2): the job takes the repository it runs in, as `pending_problems` and `mutants_outcome` already did, and the base commit as an input rather than reading it, so a fixture repository drives the whole of it — a two-crate workspace with its own `layers.toml` and an `origin/main` at the base commit, where one crate has a live function and only a pending test and the other has a live test, which is exactly the shape that made a run report the first crate's mutants caught while nothing ran. Both `live_tests_judge_every_mutant -> Ok(())` and `mutants -> Ok(())` fail that test, which is what the gate's own figures could not say before: an in-process test could not drive a job that shelled out in the process's own working directory, which is why the peer mutant was already missed on `main`. Threading the repository through put `ci` and `has_pending_tests` in the diff with untested bodies of their own, so each gained the test it was missing (an unknown job name is refused; a crate's pending markers are its own), and `cargo mutants` over the whole `xtask` diff reports 10 tested, 9 caught, 1 unviable, **0 missed**. It also makes the DEC-83 stub exemption honest rather than vacuous. That exemption is for a stub body's mutants on a run that **tested** them and found them missed, which a package with no live test never produces, so a crate of nothing but stubs is named here too and owes the gate one live test before the exemption can mean anything — #175's live tests over its error codes and vocabulary are the shape of it, and the gate's message says so rather than offering "stub the code", which such a crate has already done (independent review of this PR, finding 1). On today's workspace every safety-critical crate has live tests except `mandate-backtest` (0 live, 65 ignored), whose 18 listed mutants include the live, non-stub `BacktestError::code`, `Sign::as_str` and `AbsentStatistics::as_str`, so a PR touching `mandate-backtest/src` before E4-2 lands fails by name, which is the intended verdict; the in-flight [#163](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/163) and [#152](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/152) both pass the pre-flight | Accepted (agent, under DEC-79) | Trust-ladder rung 2 (AGENTS.md): the correction that #175 had to make by hand — writing six live tests and re-running the gate — is now a check, so the next tests PR that creates a crate cannot merge on a gate that judged nothing. The tracker's own lesson from day 2, "a diff mutation gate can pass while testing nothing (every mutant unviable)", is the same class of defect found again by a different route, and an exit code is the weakest possible evidence: nextest's 4 for "no tests to run" is indistinguishable, to `cargo-mutants`, from 100 for "a test failed". Failing rather than skipping is the conservative direction and the one DEC-137 item 3 already chose for a crate with pending tests | Make nextest's zero-test exit a pass (`-- --no-tests=pass`), which does turn such mutants into `MissedMutant` — proved by experiment — but also makes a baseline over only zero-test packages succeed where `main` fails the whole run, so it would accept a run `main` rejects; wait for a `cargo-mutants` release that reports "no tests ran" as its own outcome (27.1.0 has no such option, and the gate cannot wait on an upstream); read the outcomes after a successful run instead of listing before it (the same verdict, after paying for a run whose result is already known to be worthless); skip such a crate, as DEC-83 did (the hole DEC-137 item 3 closed); leave it to the reviewer, rung 4 (the finding on #175 came from the author running the gate by hand, which is exactly what a check is for) |
| DEC-140 | **One ES-13 exception, for the executor's submission-flow PR, and the E7-4 exemption that lets the E7-2/E7-3 slices land before E7-4** ([#174](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/174), the coordinator's rulings there). (1) **The exception.** ADR-0001 ES-13's bound of about 400 non-test src lines per PR is lifted for exactly one PR, stream K's submission flow, at **about 1,750 lines** (1,761 non-test lines measured against `main` when opened, plus 65 lines of in-module tests), because #152's oracles are end-to-end by the brief's design: the broker-side submission counter, the shadow order book rebuilt from the drafts, the shadow position ledger and the protection accountant judge the core only once intent → gate → `OrderSubmitted` → `Submit` → acknowledgment runs. The flow includes the `Unknown` machinery (silence, a duplicate id, absence confirmation over the window, spaced lookups, resubmission under the same id, and the held-intent release), because the E7-2 tests that pin the flow's age bound, abandonment and re-gate run only through it: without it the flow measured 63 missed mutants at 1,786 lines. It also folds `FillApplied` and `AccountRestrictionChanged`, which the gate reads. Everything its live tests do not pin is an `Unimplemented` stub until the slice whose tests go live with it; the flow has 0 missed mutants outside stub bodies. Every other slice stays within about 400 lines, in this order after the flow: fills and fees with the broker-initiated replacement; the account and its restrictions; reconciliation of orders, fills, supersession and the startup resume; positions, cash and fees; the agent kill switch with the confirmed cancel and the `ProtectionChanged` fold; then E7-4. **Amendment for slice 5** (the coordinator's ruling on #174, comment 5856698773): reconciliation is slice 5 at **about 470 lines** (470 non-test lines measured against slice 4) and takes positions with orders, fills, supersession and the startup resume, because positions cannot be separated from fills: every reconciliation that ingests a missing fill creates a position, so with positions left to a later slice no live test reaches the fills step, the owed compensation or the adoption fold. The cut as first listed measured 354 lines, 19 live tests and **10 missed mutants** against slice 4, closable only by contrived pins. Slice 6 then takes cash, fees and the owner acknowledgment, and clears the `buying_power` blocker; until it lands, a run with cash or fees to compare is not completed: it publishes no `ReconciliationRun`, and every agent goes `exits_only` under `reconciliation:incomplete` with the owner alerted (openings held, rule 13's exits open). So every account that has reported disables openings after its first reconciliation until slice 6, which is correct under `AGENTS.md` rule 3; slice 6's owner acknowledgment lifts it. Slice 6 completes those runs and places the restriction no more; an owner acknowledgment of `incomplete`, with step-up evidence, lifts one a stream already holds, and a completed run does not. Slice 5 measured **+499** non-test lines against main, accepted (the coordinator's ruling on #202, comment 5857629810): the overrun is the hold #202's round-1 ruling required. Its four cash-and-fee pending tests are named in `BEHAVIOUR_ONLY_TESTS` as temporary rows that expire in slice 6; each was checked to fail at its own assertion on the hold's partial answer (no cash or fee difference, an `AgentModeApplied` where a `ReconciliationRun` was expected), none at a harness panic. Slice 5 does not close an earlier window: on a fresh stream after `BrokerUpdate::Account`, and after a restart before the startup `BrokerSnapshot`, a new opening is gated and submitted before any reconciliation; the executor closes it, not the shell (the coordinator's ruling on #174, comment 5857742391): first the scoped startup hold, which holds an opening on a stream that has journaled an account until a reconciliation has run since the process started; then, before E7-7 and required for it, a harness tests-correction PR and the hold for a stream that has never journaled an account, which the backlog carries as a row that blocks E7-7. The tracer bullet relies on the executor's gate for this, never on the shell reporting the account first. The never-observed hold closes it: an opening is held until an account has been journaled and a reconciliation has run since the process started, in either order; the harness tests correction before it makes the shared test harness start that way. This amends the ordering, and is not a new exception: any later slice over about 400 lines goes back to the coordinator first. (2) **The E7-4 addendum.** The E7-2/E7-3 code submits, holds and reconciles but never places protection, so E7-4's tests fail on its absent sequence rather than at a stub. Two cheap stubs answer `Unimplemented { story: "E7-4" }`: a flatten plan handed over as an intent (in the flow) and the account or workspace kill switch (with the kill-switch slice). The pending gate checks the stub first (#194 review, round 1, finding 4), so every E7-4 test that stops at a stub needs no row: that is most of them, including the three properties #185 tightened from `prop_assume!` to `prop_assert!` and every reference case that folds a `TradingDayStarted`. The 7 that fail on the absent protective sequence instead (no bracket, OCO or stop-limit where E7-4 places one) are named one by one in `xtask`'s `BEHAVIOUR_ONLY_TESTS`: six `hand` cases and `fault::crash_between_entry_fill_and_oco`, measured on the flow. The gate still runs each and requires it to fail; a listed test that stops at a stub is reported for its row's deletion, so the list can only shrink; each row expires with E7-4, whose implementation PR deletes it with its `#[ignore]` line; and each slice's reviewer spot-checks every row, each failing for the stated reason, not on a harness panic. (3) **The intent-id properties (ruling 3).** `properties::distinct_intents_never_share_a_client_order_id` and `properties::a_client_order_id_is_a_function_of_the_intent_id_alone` draw from the full step generator, which includes fills, snapshots, external activity and the kill switch, so they go live only with the last E7-3 slice. The requirement that the flow pin the client order id's derivation is met by `hand::two_processes_derive_one_client_order_id_for_one_intent` and `hand::a_new_intent_after_a_restart_derives_a_fresh_client_order_id`, which are live in the flow: a planted 6-character truncation of the intent in `ClientOrderId::for_intent` fails both (71 live tests, 2 failed), with no flow-scoped copies of the properties. | Accepted (agent, under DEC-79; the coordinator's rulings on #174) | The alternative to one large reviewed PR was either a flow too thin for its own tests to run, which measured 10 then 63 missed mutants, or accepting missed mutants slice by slice; zero missed matters more than the line bound, and the exception is one PR, reviewed by file group with the whole flow in view. The E7-4 rows keep the pending gate's rule that every pending test runs and fails, name each exception so it cannot spread, and expire with the story | The eight-slice plan at about 400 lines each (thin slices leave live code no test reaches); all of #174 as one PR of about 3,000 lines; routing E7-4's entry points through broad stubs (it broke 29 live E7-2/E7-3 properties, whose scripts include protected entries and exits); holding every slice until E7-4 lands; flow-scoped copies of the intent-id properties (they killed no mutant and no truncation the hand tests do not already catch) |
| DEC-141 | The owner's own agent as an optional channel over the owner-input API ([strategy options](../product/10-strategy-options.md#7-recommendation), [issue #177](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/issues/177)). Mandate remains the complete product: the platform's research agent (DEC-97) is unchanged and remains the default source of ideas. As an optional channel, an owner may connect their own agent (for example Claude) through a Mandate MCP server. That server exposes the same API Mandate uses to take the owner's input; it is not a separate proposer path. Every request through it goes through the same order builder, autonomy rules, risk gate, account ledger, and journal as the owner's own input, so an LLM behind it still produces opinions, never orders (`AGENTS.md` rule 4), and the working universe still changes only as rule 11 allows. Boundaries: (1) the MCP client can never change the envelope: capital, limits, autonomy rules, allowed asset classes, `max_instruments`, signal models, and connections change only through a mandate version the human confirms with step-up (rule 11); a client may propose such a change, and only the human confirms it; (2) ASK approvals go to the human owner, and a client cannot approve its own proposal; (3) owner-only privileges stay with the human, including owner exits outside the regular session at a confirmed bid and the Stop and release choices (DEC-136), and the kill switch stays available to the human regardless of the client; (4) no broker credentials cross MCP: the client authenticates to Mandate with its own scoped, revocable token; (5) every client call is journaled with the client's identity, and the owner can revoke the client at any time; (6) no change to milestone order: the MCP server is a backlog story (E10-6) that follows the owner-input API work, after the tracer bullet (E7-7) | Accepted (founder, 2026-09-27); amended by DEC-183 (the Owlhead plugin as a primary path) and DEC-191 (a client may hold new openings), 2026-09-30 | The founder: "we should support it optionally, but ours should be complete product, it is just that they can connect it to MCP to say claude and then use same API that we would have used for taking input from user". Robinhood reported nearly 100 thousand agentic accounts on 2026-07-29 and states it does not supervise connected agents, so people who already run their own agents are a reachable on-ramp. Reusing the owner-input API adds no new route to the gate. Boundaries (1) to (6) were stated to the founder by the coordinator, and the founder did not object. The compliance posture of this channel is still for counsel (questions 31, 32, and 34). The evidence is in the [competitive landscape](../product/03-competitive-landscape.md) and [strategy options](../product/10-strategy-options.md) | A separate external-proposer path with its own admission rules (a second route to the gate to secure and review); making the owner's agent the lead product (weakens DEC-97); rejecting it (loses the on-ramp); deferring until discovery; giving outside agents broker tools directly (breaks rule 12 and journal before acting) |
| DEC-142 | **Until the personal-data vault lands, a redacted value in a `BrokerExchangeRecorded` is discarded, and its `pii_refs` entry is a deterministic positional label** ([#195](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/195), the coordinator's ruling on round 1). Journal spec §3 asks for `pii_refs` as a sorted set of opaque vault references (random IDs, never hashes of personal data), and §6.4 keeps the value in the vault. No vault exists yet, and `mandate-alpaca`'s record is a pure function (ES-21), which cannot mint a random ID. So, until **E5-5** lands: (1) the broker's `account_number`, `account_id`, and the account's own `id` are replaced in the body before it is hashed or stored, and the value is **discarded**: nothing personal is kept anywhere, including the vault; (2) each entry is `pii:<field>:<n>`, numbered in the order the fields are met, and the entries are sorted, so `pii_refs` is the sorted set §3 names in shape though not yet a vault reference; (3) a label never hashes or encodes the value. E5-5 replaces both: the value is written to the vault under a per-person key, and the entry is the vault-minted random reference, passed into the record as an input so the record stays pure. | Accepted (agent, under DEC-79; the coordinator's ruling on #195) | Discarding is the conservative side: nothing personal is kept, and the record still says which fields held personal data. A pure function cannot mint random IDs, and a hash of the value is what §3 forbids | Vaulting now (no vault exists); hashing the value into the reference (§3 forbids it); keeping the value in the journal or the artifact store (§6.4, `AGENTS.md` rule 7's spirit) |
| DEC-143 | **How the executor reads §7.3's reject-driven restrictions until the connector reject table exists** ([#198](https://github.com/kunwarshivam/mandate/pull/198), the coordinator's ruling on round 1). (1) **Closing-only detection.** §7.3's second row reads a reject "whose code or message indicates closing-only or restricted trading (connector reject table)", and §15 open question 4 leaves that table undecided: no connector maps a reject `code` to a restriction today, so `mandate-executor` reads the **message** only, lower-cased, for the substrings `closing` or `restricted`, and journals `code` on `RejectObserved` for the table to read once it exists. Free-text matching over-triggers: an order-level message such as "position is restricted for this symbol" puts **every** agent on the account in `exits_only`. That is accepted as the conservative side (`AGENTS.md` rule 3): `exits_only` never denies an exit (rule 13), and the restriction holds until the owner acknowledges it and the account is refreshed (§7.3, the reconciliation slices' owner acknowledgment). (2) **The 403 run.** §7.3's third row counts "N consecutive 403 rejects without a known order-level cause". The executor counts **every** 403, one that names one of our orders with a code included, as a deliberate stricter reading until the reject table can say which codes are an order-level cause: N order-level 403s in a row then restrict the account to closing only, which again only holds openings. A non-403 reject, and any restriction change, starts the run afresh, and the account is asked for once, when the run restricts it. | Accepted (agent, under DEC-79; the coordinator's ruling on #198) | Both readings err toward holding openings, never toward adding risk, and the owner acknowledgment lifts them; the connector reject table replaces both with codes | Reading `code` against an invented table (§15 question 4 is open); exempting a named-order 403 now (it needs the table to know which codes are order-level) |

The rule 11 sentence states what DEC-141 already implies, so an agent reading the rules cannot treat an owner-connected agent as a runtime source of theses | Veto any entry (reopens its brief); leave rule 11's text as it was |
| DEC-145 | **No loss or breach guarantee.** Mandate does not pay users' losses or the cost of a mandate breach, whether insured or self-funded; no plan, page, or sales material offers such a guarantee.
